Esta semana cubrió: salida probabilística de la neurona logística, pérdida de log-verosimilitud negativa, entrenamiento con descenso de gradiente estocástico, clasificación multiclase one-vs-rest y regularización L1/L2 para limitar el efecto de outliers y evitar sobreajuste.

> One idea that stood out to me this week is that logistic regression can be useful in water-distribution SCADA systems even when the final engineering decision should not rely only on a 0.5 threshold. For example, recent pressure, flow, tank-level, and actuator-state measurements could be used to estimate the probability that the system is in an abnormal operating condition.
>
> The discussion about outliers and regularization seems especially important in this setting. A single sensor error, communication failure, or mislabeled event could influence a linear decision boundary. Regularization can help prevent a model from assigning excessively large importance to one noisy signal. L1 regularization could also help identify a smaller subset of informative variables, although I would still validate whether an excluded SCADA variable has operational relevance.
>
> I also think the probability output is more useful than a hard classification alone. In a water utility, the decision threshold should depend on the consequence of a missed event versus the cost of a false alarm.
>
> How would you choose and justify a classification threshold when abnormal events are rare and false alarms can overload operators?

> What I found most useful this week was the connection between the logistic neuron and probability. The sigmoid function makes the output continuous, so instead of only making a yes-or-no decision, the model estimates how likely it is that a point belongs to a class.
>
> I also found the discussion of regularization helpful. A model with very large weights can be overly sensitive to outliers or small changes in the input data. Adding an L1 or L2 penalty gives the model a preference for smaller weights, which can help it generalize better to unseen data. L1 regularization is especially interesting because it can reduce some weights to zero and effectively remove less useful features.
>
> One question I still have is: when a regularized model performs slightly worse on training data but better on test data, what is the best way to decide how much regularization is appropriate?

Es una participación natural, toca probabilidad, sobreajuste, outliers, L1/L2 y abre una pregunta para que otros respondan.
